Current Muse Scores 47% on Meta’s Personal Superintelligence Index

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Meta AI Research announced on November 6, 2026 that it is rolling out specialized versions of the models behind Muse and Meta AI, trained using Muse Wonder, with the current Muse model scoring 47% on its Personal Superintelligence Index versus 38% for the previous model.

The update brings Active Memory and Active Focus to both products. The models being deployed are specialized versions of Meta’s existing systems, improved through Muse Wonder, rather than a rollout of the Wonder series itself. That approach extends capabilities introduced with Wonder to more people through the assistants’ existing models.

Active Memory lets the assistants retain important information and retrieve it when it becomes relevant to a later conversation. Training covers the difference between temporary and permanent information, reasoning over time, and recognizing when details remain relevant or have become stale. This makes memory a problem of selection and timing: the model must identify which earlier context applies now and which has gone stale.

For the improved Muse models, Meta reports a 2.4x reduction in forgetting information critical to users’ goals. Its Active Memory Eval shows the current model correctly using critical context in 90% of tasks, compared with 76% for the previous model. Those figures describe separate parts of the problem: retaining information needed for a goal and applying that context correctly during a task.

Active Focus is intended to keep Muse moving toward a goal when an unexpected challenge interrupts the work. Meta trained the models on varied bottlenecks designed to reflect real-world conditions so they can persist, recover, or pursue alternatives for the user. In Muse, Meta reports a 67% improvement in completing everyday tasks where unexpected issues arise.

The specialized models also address how an agent works with someone while a task is underway. They are designed to multitask, choose between independent progress and live collaboration, and incorporate steering or clarifications without becoming confused or losing the task. That links persistence to responsiveness: an agent can continue pursuing the goal while still changing course when the user intervenes.

In Meta AI, the same improvements are intended to make the assistant a better thinking partner and collaborator. It can divide complex problems into clear steps, ask sharper follow-up questions, and keep pace when a user changes direction mid-conversation.

For users, the rollout’s practical test is continuity: whether useful context survives changing circumstances, work recovers from obstacles, and corrections can be incorporated without losing the goal. Those behaviors connect Meta’s reported results to the everyday collaboration it intends to support in Muse and Meta AI.

The PokÃ©mon TCG: 30th Celebration Figure Collection was released today. It’s one of many highly sought-after boxed products from the ongoing throwback collection, and many of those desperate to snag a box stood in line overnight in order for a chance to get one. That included at least one dad who took his daughter to a Target in Charleston, South Carolina after midnight. She was only six years old. “Witnesses told police that sometime after 1 a.m., [the man] gave his daughter a pillow and blanket before leaving her asleep in line,” reported local broadcast affiliate Live 5 WCSC.

She was unharmed but has been placed in emergency protective custody with the South Carolina Department of Social Services.

Underkeep is my favorite old-school dungeon crawler since Legend of Grimrock, and yes that includes Queen's Domain

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Queen's Domain might be the trendy game of the moment but for my money, the classic grid-based action of Underkeep is where it's at. (And yes, there's a demo.)

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Everyone's talking about Queen's Domain right now, but as someone who tried Queen's Domain and did not care for it, allow me to direct your attention to an alternative old-school dungeon crawler: Underkeep, which launched on Steam on November 5 and has been absolutely phenomenal through the roughly four hours I've put into it so far.

Like Queen's Domain, Underkeep is firmly rooted in the 1990s, but I'm talkin' the early '90s, and even before that: Classic grid-based RPGs like The Bard's Tale, Dungeon Master, Wizardry, and Eye of the Beholder. In fact, as someone who loves Eye of the Beholder (specifically the second one, The Legend of Darkmoon), I noticed a lot of nearly direct lifts from that series: primarily visual, but also a few audio cues, that I swear I've heard before.

But my concerns that Underkeep might be a low-effort knockoff evaporated pretty quickly. After four hours of play I feel like I've barely scratched the game's surface: I still haven't found the titular village of Underkeep, where I assume the real action will begin, but I've crashed (literally, in a few cases) through multiple dungeons, all interconnected horizontally or vertically, and explored an overworld that's a whole lot bigger than I expected. At the very least, Underkeep looks beefy.

Underkeep's gameplay preserves the vibe of those old-time RPGs while eliminating most of their more objectionable nonsense. Its shared inventory is divided into categories that make managing and swapping equipment between party members extremely easy, the automapping is detailed while still allowing freehand notes (I know, I know, get some paper and do your own maps, but who has time for that these days?), and tools are used automatically without needing to be equipped.

And unlike so many games in the genre, Underkeep is entirely turn-based. This eliminates the cheese strategy of hitting an enemy and moving a step away before they can hit back, which carried so many gamers through so many fights in so many games over the years. But it also encourages more thoughtfulness: When I see an enemy in the distance, I can take my time to think about what I want to do, move to a more advantageous position, or even just go take a break for a while.

I'm free to do whatever I want until melee combat actually begins. Once that happens, everyone in the party has to finish their turn, and then let the enemies finish theirs, at which point we're all free to run away (and probably be chased) or launch another round of fighting. I'll always come down on the real-time-with-pause side of the argument for isometric RPGs, but the turn-based approach is ideal for Underkeep.

Character classes are a fairly standard mix—fighter, paladin, wizard, thief, that sort of thing—but the races are a bit more interesting. Humans and dwarves are on the menu, and "elk" are kind of like elves, but then you get the stoneborn, raven, and slugman, all of them nicely weird. Stoneborn, for instance, are basically golems⁠—extremely strong and tough, but they have the agility of a Mack truck and can't wear body armor. They're basically a boulder that can hit you really hard, which is great in some ways but has some real drawbacks too. Ravens, meanwhile, are subject to random mutations that can take them off in unforeseeable directions: My own raven character recently mutated a healing buff called Roommate from the Void: "Something moved into your body at some point and never left. It pays rent in blood."

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Slugmen, well, they're just slugs who are also men. They look like this:

There's also no limit to combining races and classes: A stoneborn thief or slugman paladin may not be a great idea, but if you want to give it a shot, knock yourself out.

There's not a lot of reading to do in Underkeep, but the writing for those encounters is pleasingly evocative in an old-school tabletop way, and there are plenty of surprises to be had too. Depending on how you deal with the red-cloaked guy above, for instance, you may get a reward, you might get jumped, or you could find yourself facing some sort of bigger trouble down the road—that's not confirmed yet, but I definitely got some 'Be seein' you' stinkeye from him before he slunk off into the next life.

It makes me a little sad to say, but one of my first thoughts when I saw Underkeep was whether the assets were made with generative AI. I'm not the only one to have that worry: Multiple arguments about it are underway in the Steam forums right now. Unlike some people in those conversations who say they "can just tell" when AI is present, I cannot—but the Steam store page has no AI disclosure, and developer Rake in Grass has repeatedly insisted that no generative AI was used.

"All artwork in Underkeep was hand-drawn by our artists," an FAQ states. "Some variations in style are natural, as several artists contributed to the game. The occasional "mixels" are caused by dynamic scaling used to simulate 3D perspective and smooth animations."

It's unfortunate that debates like this are so commonplace now, because Underkeep is a joy: I haven't played a whole huge lot of them in recent years, but this is easily my favorite grid-based RPG since the great Legend of Grimrock 2, which is now 12 years old. (Quick correction: The original Grimrock will soon be 15, Grimrock 2 isn't quite there yet. Thanks to Sebastian for letting me know.)

Underkeep is out now on Steam for $19.50, a 15% launch discount on the regular price that's available until November 19. You can also sample the goods by way of the demo, which offers about an hour of highly compressed gameplay and some fun surprises.

From there he graduated to the glory days of Sierra Online adventures and Microprose sims, ran a local BBS, learned how to build PCs, and developed a longstanding love of RPGs, immersive sims, and shooters. He began writing videogame news in 2007 for The Escapist and somehow managed to avoid getting fired until 2014, when he joined the storied ranks of PC Gamer. He covers all aspects of the industry, from new game announcements and patch notes to legal disputes, Twitch beefs, esports, and Henry Cavill. Lots of Henry Cavill.

Whitney Museum selects a staff curator and a stage director to lead 2028 biennial

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Meg Onli and Zack Winokur Photo by Bryan Derballa

The Whitney Museum of American Art has selected Meg Onli, a curator at the museum, and Zack Winokur, a choreographer and director for the stage, to curate the 2028 edition of the Whitney Biennial. It is due to open in March 2028.

“Every Whitney Biennial springs from a distinct curatorial alchemy,” Scott Rothkopf, the Whitney’s director, said in a statement. “Meg is one of the most compelling curatorial voices working today, with an extraordinary ability to champion artists and bring fresh perspectives to the history of American art. Zack is an equally inventive artistic programmer who has become one of the leading voices of his generation through groundbreaking work across opera, theater, dance and the visual arts. Their partnership promises a radical new vision for the 2028 Biennial.”

Onli is one of the Whitney’s star curators, having joined the institution as a curator-at-large in 2023, then as a curator in 2024. She co-curated the 2024 Whitney Biennial, titled Even Better Than the Real Thing, with fellow Whitney curator Chrissie Iles. Most recently she co-curated the museum’s current Roy Lichtenstein retrospective (until 10 May 2027).

“From the Whitney’s early Annuals to its many subsequent iterations, the Biennial has a rich history of reinvention that is already giving us much to think about,” Onli said in a statement. “I couldn’t be more excited to explore what the Biennial can be alongside Zack, who is such a dynamic thinker, and to see where our conversations with artists lead.”

Winokur’s work spans dance, music, visual art, opera and theatre. He is the co-founder and artistic director of the performing arts collective AMOC* (American Modern Opera Company), which last year staged a five-week takeover of the Lincoln Center campus on Manhattan’s Upper West Side. He was previously the artistic director of Little Island, a park and cultural event space designed by Heatherwick Studio and MNLA that rises out of the Hudson River not far from the Whitney. His projects for the stage and screen have included directing productions at Opera Philadelphia and the Santa Fe Opera, and working on the expanded version of The Wizard of Oz currently showing at Sphere in Las Vegas.

“I’m honoured to be curating the Whitney Biennial with Meg,” Winokur said in a statement. “Our shared desire to centre objects and people and the collision between them already echoes our collaboration. It’s that energy that makes the dialogue with her so fun and inspiring. As an artist, what excites me most is being in service to other artists, and I’m delighted to have the opportunity to do so in the context of the Whitney.”

The 2026 Whitney Biennial, which was organised by staff curators Marcela Guerrero and Drew Sawyer, closed in October. It was seen by more than 550,000 visitors, according to the museum’s figures, around 75% of whom identified as either Millennials or Gen Z, making it the youngest audience since the Whitney started tracking visitors’ ages.

The exhibition’s 2026 edition was also remarkably controversy-free, though the exhibition is often a lightning rod for debates, protests and political statements. The 2019 edition was led by Whitney curators Jane Panetta and Rujeko Hockley, and came to be known colloquially as the “tear gas biennial” due to weekly protests and participating artists’ threatened withdrawals over the Whitney’s vice chairman at the time, Warren Kanders, who was the chief executive of Safariland, a defense and security contractor that manufactured tear gas canisters that had been deployed against asylum-seekers at the Mexico-US border, among other materials. Kanders eventually resigned from the museum’s board; he is currently the chairman and chief executive of Cadre Holdings, a Florida-based manufacturer of security and law-enforcement equipment.

The 2017 Whitney Biennial, which was co-curated by Christopher Y. Lew and Mia Locks, was largely overshadowed by the wide-ranging debate over a single painting in the exhibition: Dana Schutz’s Open Casket (2016), in which the artist portrayed the famous image from 1955 of 14-year-old Emmett Till lying in an open casket following his brutal abduction, mutilation and murder. The artist Hannah Black wrote an open letter criticising the work’s inclusion in the biennial and calling for its destruction, sparking protests inside and outside the museum and arguments about censorship, identity and cultural appropriation.

The 2028 Whitney Biennial will be the 83rd edition of the exhibition, which started out as a series of medium-specific exhibitions before becoming the Whitney Annual in 1937, then transitioning to its every-other-year format in 1973. It remains the most closely watched recurring exhibition of contemporary art in the Americas.

The Whitney Museum has turned to two in-house curators to put together a show that celebrates diversity in American art—but as in 2017, the biennial is already mired in controversy

The 82nd edition of the most closely-watched recurring exhibition in the United States will open in spring 2026

After a 2022 Biennial curated entirely in-house, the Whitney has selected one staff member, Iles, and an independent curator, Onli, to organise the exhibition’s 81st edition

The 8 Best Sweater Polos for Men, According to Style Editors

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

Your next best-dressed moment starts from the collar down.

I CONSIDER MYSELF the king of sweaters. I love a good collared shirt, so polos are my go-to in the spring and summer. But once fall and winter roll around, I’m all about sweaters. Even better: a sweater polo. It’s the perfect mashup that combines the collar and button placket of a classic polo with the knit construction of a sweater, giving you a little more texture and warmth than your standard cotton pique. The best sweater polos offer that same versatility. You can dress things up or lean super casual depending on the day and occasion. And not all have to be long-sleeved either.

Short-sleeve versions are also great for tropical vacations or layers under a blazer for the office. Either way, they’re an easy way to switch up your usual sweater rotation from the same ol’ crews or turtlenecks. The Men’s Health fashion crew and I have tried more than 30 over the last three years, paying close attention to fit, fabric, comfort, and versatility to find ones that deserve a spot in your closet.

For the past five years, I’ve tested more than 25 sweater polos, but Uniqlo is easily one of my favorites. It checks all the boxes when it comes to excellent quality, fit, versatility and what really seals the deal—affordability. At just $50, you’re getting a premium knit that could pass for something more expensive. It’s made from a cotton-blend yarn in a structured Milano rib knit that looks and feels more like woven cloth than a stretchy tee. This one feels dense yet comfortable on the skin. I especially love the buttonless, open V-neck collar, which gives some breathing room and makes the whole thing feel a little relaxed.

In terms of fit, it’s roomy through the chest and flexible through the arms without looking oversized. I’ve worn it with jeans and dressed it up with tailored trousers and loafers for dinner. Plus, it comes in plenty of colors that you can rotate between office days and the weekend. After more than three wash cycles, it’s held up nicely. Just be sure to toss it in a mesh laundry bag to keep the knit in good shape.

You don’t have to spend a fortune to get your hands on a good sweater polo. Check out this one from Coofandy. It’s one of the lighter knits that I’ve tested, so it’s wearable through all the seasons. Yes, even in the summertime when the temps stayed around 80 degrees, I didn’t break a sweat at the backyard party. The polyester fabric feels soft against the skin and has a little stretch, which makes it easy to move around in.

It also has a V-neck polo collar to give it that laid-back vibe, while the ribbed cuffs and hem pull everything in for a more fitted, body-contouring look. Styling-wise, you’ve got plenty of options. I’d wear it untucked with jeans or chinos for an everyday fit, but you can also tuck it in pants with a belt for a day on the golf course. This one is not machine washable, so I’d recommend taking a trip to the dry cleaners.

I love mixing different textures in my polo sweater collection, so things don’t start looking too plain. That’s when I add in pieces like Charles Tyrwhitt’s textured knit to give some depth and dimension to my outfit without going overboard with loud patterns or prints. It’s made from 100 percent cotton that’s been garment-washed for a softer, broken-in feel, so I’m not dealing with a stiff, scratchy sweater.

The midweight material is substantial enough to wear on its own during cooler days but breathable enough to layer under a jacket without feeling too bundled up. I’d wear the petrol-blue shade with cream trousers and brown suede loafers for a sharp office look, or pair it with dark denim and boots for dinner. I’ve washed it more than four times and haven’t seen it shrink or pill, but my advice is to let it air dry.

When my brain can’t function in the morning to put an outfit together, I just rely on Abercrombie’s knit. It’s made from 100 percent merino wool, which sounds like it might be a recipe for itchiness, but surprisingly, it feels soft and smooth against the skin. I also dig the classic three-button collar, which gives it a slightly dressier edge than your average crewneck. The fit is also a nice balance, with enough room in the chest and stomach, in case I wolfed down a burger.

Now, I will say that the wool can get a little toasty, especially if you’re moving around a lot. But if your office blasts AC like it’s the middle of Antarctica, you should be just fine. Styling-wise, I’d wear it with straight-leg jeans and sneakers for a casual Friday, or swap in chinos and loafers when I want to look a little more put-together. It only comes in neutrals, which should be easy to pair with anything in your wardrobe.

There’s something about cashmere that makes even the simplest outfit feel a little more luxurious. And if you’re going to invest in a cashmere sweater polo, Quince is a great place to start. This one is made from 100 percent Grade-A Mongolian cashmere, with fine fibers that give the knit an incredibly soft, smooth feel. The 12-gauge construction gives it a refined look without making it feel overly chunky, and the ribbed cuffs and hem help keep its shape. And when I don’t want to wear a dress shirt, this is a great alternative. Throw it on with tailored trousers and loafers for the office, or pair it with dark jeans and boots for dinner.

I’m a big fan of merino wool because it gives you the warmth of a sweater minus all the extra bulk. Banana Republic uses it for this polo, which is a solid option that can transition from the office straight to dinner. No matter how toasty a room gets, I’m never sweating in it. The fabric stays lightweight and breathable, and has enough warmth to come in handy when temperatures drop.

I also like that the fit is pretty straightforward, with slightly dropped shoulders and ribbed cuffs and hem that give it a relaxed but clean finish. The buttoned collar makes it feel a little dressier than your average knit, especially when paired with chinos or tailored trousers. And unlike some wool sweaters that require a trip to the dry cleaner, this one is machine washable, which is always a plus in my book.

Cos gave the classic sweater polo a completely different spin with a buttonless collar that sits closed rather than opening into the usual V-neck. It’s a subtle detail, but one that makes people take a second look at my outfit. This neckline creates a cleaner, more sophisticated look without being overly flashy. I’d pair it with wide-leg trousers and leather loafers for a sharp office fit, or throw it on with relaxed jeans and sneakers for the weekend.

I especially like this kind of silhouette for guys who prefer a minimalist wardrobe but still want something with a little personality. Now, at around $130, it’s definitely on the pricier side, especially when you can find sweater polos for half that amount. But I’d argue you’re paying for a design that’s a refreshing departure from the sweater polos you’re used to seeing, and chances are, you won’t spot five other guys wearing the same thing.

Not every sweater polo needs long sleeves, especially when you can rock one under a blazer. Take this retro-inspired short-sleeve style from Bonobos, which I can wear year-round. My favorite way to style it is under a structured blazer with tailored trousers. It’s dressy enough to skip the traditional button-down shirt, whether you’re heading to an important meeting or a cocktail reception.

It’s also a great piece to pack for vacation, especially if you’re traveling somewhere tropical and need something sharp for a resort dinner. In that case, ditch the blazer and pair it with linen pants or even shorts for a more laid-back look. Plus, the breathable cotton fabric won’t make scorching temps feel even more unbearable. I wore it on a recent trip to Cancún and didn’t break much of a sweat. Depending on your personal style, Bonobos offers this sweater polo in a variety of colors, prints, and textures, so there’s plenty to choose from.

The Comfy Pants BA Editors Are Wearing to Thanksgiving

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Thanksgiving is my favorite holiday, not an uncommon feeling around the BA offices. I love the planning, the days (yes: days plural) spent preparing the feast in the kitchen alongside my mom, the gathering of loved ones around a table to reflect, savor, and enjoy. There is one thing, however, that I don’t love: The moment after dinner and before dessert in which I become so full that I must excuse myself and retire to the couch, where I clutch a pillow to my stomach until I’ve digested enough to reemerge.

As somewhat of an expert in feeling Too Full and Bloated™, I can attest that this feeling is made infinitely worse when I’m wearing rigid, tight-waisted pants. Perhaps you can relate? While stretchy sweatpants are an obvious solution here, there are only so many meal scenarios in which it’s socially acceptable to wear them. For those other occasions—the holiday feasts, celebratory meals out, and thousand-course tasting menu dinners—I’ve made it my mission to fill my wardrobe with pants that look polished and feel comfortable, no matter how many helpings of stuffing or “fries for the table” I’ve had. Here are my tried-and-true favorite pairs for eating big meals in, along with my fellow food-loving colleagues’ go-tos.

I was a die-hard high-waist pants gal for the majority of my life. Then I tried Still Here’s Everyday Jeans. A true mid-rise, they hit just above my hip bones and about an inch below my belly button, hugging my body without digging in, even after a holiday dinner or multicourse restaurant meal. The non-stretch denim is soft but sturdy, and the relaxed leg is structured without feeling at all rigid. I own them in Washed Black and Classic Blue and wear them constantly, to everything from casual weeknight dinners (paired with sneakers and a white tee) to weekend date nights (with kitten heels and a silk top). They’ve even become my go-to Friendsgiving uniform in recent years because of how comfortable they are to cook, eat, and host in.

If you haven’t already heard of Rag & Bone’s Miramar line, let me put you on: These pants look like real denim, but are actually made from soft cotton terry. It’s all thanks to the brand’s printing technique, which allows them to digitally print details like weave, stitching, buttons, and pockets onto any fabric. Ever since I got my first pair, I’ve been delighted every time I receive a compliment on my jeans and can reveal that they’re actually—gasp—sweatpants! I’ve owned the Sofie style for a few years now, which have real pockets and a true button fly that give them a more polished look; but my recent obsession are the Sport Stripe Track Pants, which have a flattering wide-leg cut and a fully elastic waistband that makes them ideal for multicourse meals.

I invested in a pair of poplin pants from Suzie Kondi this summer and they’ve quickly become my go-to attire whenever I want the comfort of an elastic waistband while still looking put-together. The low-rise waist sits comfortably on my hips and ensures that nothing squeezes my midsection, and the straight leg cut is incredibly flattering. The cotton poplin fabric is breathable and feels quite luxe, and I’ve even noticed that stains come out surprisingly easily (which I learned the hard way by spilling green juice all over myself testing personal blenders). Forever a fan of a monochrome ’fit, I love pairing mine with the matching Vella shirt.

If you’re looking for a more affordable option, J.Crew’s Luna Pants have gained a well-deserved following on social media this year. They’re just as stylish as the pricier brands, available in several colors, and ring in at under $100.

Many of the above pants feel like sweatpants, but if you’re enjoying a casual dinner, there’s nothing stopping you from wearing actual sweatpants—particularly if you opt for an elevated pair that ditches the typical brushed fleece for cashmere or velour in a straight-leg cut. Senior reviews editor Olivia Tarantino and I are both fans of Quince’s Mongolian Cashmere Straight Leg Pants for their ultrasoft feel and flattering cut. If you’re looking to upgrade to something more luxe, executive audience development director Casey Hendricks vouches for the cashmere Everywhere Pant from Guest in Residence: “They’re THE pants for when you can’t fathom wearing actual pants. They’re comfortable but structured for sweatpants, and they’re super warm. I’ve had these pants for years and they still feel and look good as new.” I’m also partial to Suzie Kondi’s Lito Pants, which are cut from cozy velour and have a slightly flared leg that looks good on everyone. While I don’t own these myself, I steal my mom’s pair every time I’m home to visit (sorry, Mom!).

100% Washable Silk Drawstring Wide Leg Pants

For dressier dinners, I like to opt for a pair of slinky satin pants. The elastic waistband and flowy fabric ensures they stay comfy even after the meal is said and done, while the shine and elegant drape make them easy to dress up. Hendricks calls La Ligne’s Colby Pants her “everything pants.” She says she wears them for “work, errands, eating, airport, and everything in between. They’re so comfortable but also so chic—they make me feel effortlessly put-together.” I also love Quince’s 100% Washable Silk Drawstring Wide Leg Pants, which have an impressive sub-$100 price tag and are (as the name suggests) magically machine washable.

Long gone are the days where I’ve been an athleisure-on-the-plane kind of girl. In this job (and in life in general), I've started to prefer clothes that are both comfortable and acceptable to wear to a restaurant meal straight from the plane. Enter the Reformation Olina Linen Pants. They’re perhaps the only pants that I’d wear on an 11-hour travel day straight into a few-hours-long dinner, and in fact, I have. The linen gives them an elevated look, but the drawstring waist and just-soft-enough fabric make them easy to sit in for a long while. I’m tempted to order them in every color, but my singular pair has lasted many trips, many meals, and many washes thus far. —Kate Kassin, editorial operations manager

As an adult man approaching 40, I want a pair of pants just wide enough to convey I’m in the know without going full MC Hammer. Enter the Uniqlo pleated, relaxed wide pants. With an elastic, drawstring waistband and belt loops, I get to dine out free from navel constrictions and still wear a smart belt. I can go from the office to the bistro to the couch without sacrificing comfort or functionality. To you, the casual observer, though, I look like I’m wearing slacks. With the loose, high-waist fit, these pants drape beautifully with a pair of black leather penny loafers. They’re made of 67% recycled polyester, which feels virtuous and breezy. —Gabe Hiatt, senior restaurants editor

I am content with my daily uniform of selvedge jeans, leather shoes, and a dark T-shirt or sweater depending on the weather. It’s not too formal for working in a kitchen, but acceptable should I find myself in an elevator with someone from Vogue. But running around town all day, with the occasional epic dinner out, means a constant trade-off between style and comfort. Whoever designed 100% cotton jeans for railroad workers and miners just didn’t consider the rigors of a 3-hour meal followed by a long train ride home. So lately I have been quietly upgrading my look with Naked and Famous Denim’s stretch selvedge jeans. They contain 2% elastane (perhaps you know it as Spandex), just enough to provide a forgiving fit, but with the look and patina of raw denim. They stay conformable enough all day that you can simply ignore them and think about more important things: like dinner. —Chris Morocco, food director

There is no worse feeling than the cold, hard button of a stiff jean pressing against your full stomach after a particularly big meal. (Who designed jeans anyway?). That’s why for Thanksgiving there won’t be a firm, oppressive waistband in sight. I already know I’ll be donning my Le Bon Shoppe Arc Pants, not only for their loose barrel-leg silhouette, but also for their forgiving elasticated waist band. Namely, more pie for me, without the feeling of “pants” up against my indulgent belly. Pair with a festive cashmere sweater (preferably oversized) and you can go from the dinner table straight to the couch for your turkey-induced coma in both style and comfort. —June Kim, executive editor

As someone who’s no stranger to a mid-meal top-button release, I’ve developed a deep appreciation for an elastic waistband. Even better when it comes in a pair of pants I’m excited to wear. My current favorite: J.Crew’s Luna pants in lightweight corduroy. A more fall-friendly take on the poplin version above, these are like sweatpants disguised as corduroys, with plenty of room for big Thanksgiving ambitions while still making you feel put-together. Honestly, what more could you ask of a pant?

You can dress them up with a sweater and loafers or keep it casual with a T-shirt and sneakers. I’ve worn them so often that I’m already plotting a second pair in a different color. My one caveat: They tend to run a little big, so I’d suggest sizing down. —Gabby Fowler, social media director

PSA Pilots Secure Contract Extension Through 2030

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

PSA Airlines’ pilot union leaders have approved an agreement that extends the regional carrier’s pilot contract by two years and locks in three rounds of pay increases.

The Air Line Pilots Association announced the agreement Wednesday. It extends the existing contract through April 1, 2030. The previous extension, ratified in 2022, had set an amendable date of April 1, 2028.

Current pay rates will remain in place until June 1, 2027, when the first increases take effect. Additional raises are scheduled for June 1 in each of the following two years. The deal also changes scheduling and work rules. ALPA’s announcement did not specify the size of the raises or detail the scheduling changes.

“When we asked our pilots what mattered most, the answer was clear: Protect our pay. This agreement does that,” said Capt. Chris Sabia, who chairs the union’s PSA Master Executive Council, in a news release.

ALPA represents approximately 2,260 pilots at PSA, which is a wholly-owned regional subsidiary of American Airlines.

How Stephen Miller Schemed His Way to the Top

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump on Friday announced that Stephen Miller, one of his most powerful and politically aggressive aides, would be his next White House chief of staff following disastrous results for Republicans in the midterm elections.

Miller, whose titles until now were deputy chief of staff for policy and homeland security adviser, has orchestrated the most polarizing policies of Trump’s second term—most notably a brutal crackdown on immigration that has claimed the lives of US citizens.

Trump made the change public in a statement on his Truth Social platform, writing that Miller would take over from Susie Wiles on Monday—less than a week after Republicans lost the House and Senate in a searing rebuke of Trump’s policy agenda.

In recent days, Trump has privately blamed Wiles for the worst Republican midterm performance in decades, according to two sources familiar with the matter. Wiles submitted her resignation on Thursday, the sources say.

Trump broadly considered three candidates to replace Wiles, the sources say: special envoy Steve Witkoff, director of national intelligence Jay Clayton, and Miller.

But with the looming threat of congressional investigations starting next year, Trump settled on Miller, seemingly because of his zeal in defending the president and his deep loyalty. Miller has been with Trump since his first campaign and stood by him through his political exile in 2021.

Miller had made his interest in the job known inside the West Wing for weeks, ever since Wiles weathered a series of negative stories before the midterms that suggested her job was at risk, the sources say.

In recent months, Trump also started cutting Wiles out of certain decisions, which contributed to the sense among her detractors that she was on shaky ground, sources say. For instance, Trump reportedly hired Katie Zacharia as press secretary without consulting Wiles, though the White House pushed back on the claim.

With Wiles’ departure, White House aides were bracing for further personnel changes. Among other names, Wiles’ deputies James Blair, who put together the GOP midterm strategy, and Richard Walters are seen as vulnerable to being fired by Miller unless they resign first, sources say.

The changing of the guard is also likely to bring respite to defense secretary Pete Hegseth, who has had a strained relationship with Wiles as of late but is seen as ideologically aligned with Miller, sources say.

Hegseth and Miller worked closely last year when the Trump administration began a campaign of strikes against boats around Venezuela. Miller, who also leads the Homeland Security Council within the White House, orchestrated the campaign through the Pentagon.

Wiles leaves the White House as arguably the most successful chief of staff to Trump, who infamously cycled through chiefs in his first term. By comparison, Wiles kept Trump’s confidence from the start of his second presidential campaign in 2021.

Still, Wiles weathered a number of challenges in recent months. She underwent a successful course of chemotherapy, but also faced accusations that her daughter had received lucrative contracts in part because of the family’s connection to the president. (The White House and Wiles’ daughter’s firm pushed back on these claims.)

“Serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime,” Wiles wrote on X. “I’m so proud to have been a part of what we’ve accomplished together from securing our border and cutting taxes to strengthening our economy and putting America First.”

Ray-Ban Meta Audio review: Great specs, shame about the price

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses attempt to curb the controversy by removing the cameras, and while they’re still an excellent product, the price makes them harder to recommend.

This has been a bit of a rollercoaster year for Meta wearables. Meta’s VR division shrank considerably earlier this year, then later announced a massively redesigned follow-up that’ll launch next year. And while Meta has debuted dozens of new smart glasses styles throughout the year that have sold quite well, the company can’t seem to get away from controversy. That’s where Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses come in.

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses do exactly what they sound like. It’s a pair of smart glasses powered by Meta AI and only includes the audio part of the Ray-Ban Meta experience. There are exactly zero cameras on these glasses, and the hope (obviously) is that they’ll provide a smart experience without the potential privacy problems. The problem, for me at least, is that they’re simply too expensive despite lacking standout features.

Now, that doesn’t mean Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses aren’t worthwhile. They feature a much wider prescription range (-12 to +6) than the majority of Meta smart glasses models, which often cap out at -6 to +6. The battery life is also significantly better thanks to the lack of a camera and other improvements in the hardware. And, of course, if you want a pair of AI glasses but don’t want the camera, these are the only options from Meta.

If I were to choose between the two available models, I’d pick the Clubmaster style every time. Not only do they look better overall and feature the thinnest frames of any Meta smart glasses, but they also have substantially better nosepads that are adjustable and can be easily replaced.

The Burbank model looks just fine, but the nosepads aren’t the same, and the glasses feel more rigid overall (which I find less comfortable). Both models come in lots of colors with many different lens options, including clear, tinted, polarized, Transitions, and prescription.

Both models feature a touchpad on the outside of the right temple arm, as well as a small, circular button up top to activate Meta AI. You can alternatively use the “Hey, Meta” keywords to activate Meta AI. The button and touchpad can be disabled if you like. Only the touchpad has an alternative function, and that alternative function only controls music playback via Spotify or Amazon Music. Other Meta glasses offer far more options.

Both styles are the lightest Meta glasses available. Most Meta smart glasses with cameras weigh in around the 54g mark. Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses weigh just 44g. They’re not as light as Even Realities G2, RayNeo iO, or MemoMind One, but 44g is still considered pretty light for smart glasses.

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses reuse the case from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 2, which looks more like a traditional Ray-Ban case rather than the foldable one that ships with Meta Ray-Ban Display or the 2026 Meta Camera Glasses. I don’t have an issue with Ray-Ban Meta Audio’s case at all, but I really enjoy the foldable one and wish it were included.

Audio playback on these is great, and while the speakers are identical to all other Meta glasses products, using them as spatial surround sound “headphones” while watching a movie with surround sound is a fantastic experience. They also have spatial audio recording and truly excellent noise cancellation thanks to the 6-mic setup.

But the lack of the newer processor from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 3 means the glasses don’t support the new hearing aid function. To me, that’s a massive missed opportunity since these are audio-only glasses. When I asked Meta about it, they only said it wasn’t available on Ray-Ban Meta Audio for now, so this problem could be fixed in an upcoming update.

And that’s actually where Meta’s glasses might shine brightest: software updates. The company has been exemplary with update cadence on all of its products, and its smart glasses constantly get new, innovative features that surprise and delight. While I’m a little underwhelmed with the number of features available on these glasses today, this product will likely look quite different a year from now, a fact that’s backed up by years of Meta significantly improving product feature sets over time.

If you buy these glasses today, know that you’re mainly investing in them for the future. They’re fine enough for today’s usage, especially if you’re interested in a hands-free way to use Meta AI products like Muse or just want an open-ear way to listen to music and take phone calls, but Meta’s penchant for continually adding new features is, undoubtedly, going to be the highlight for everyone who ever buys them.

Kourtney Kardashian Slams ‘Fabricated’ Rumors That She Doesn’t Live With Travis Barker and Shares ‘Really Vulnerable’ Renovation of Calabasas Home

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Kourtney Kardashian is setting the record straight on her living arrangements with her husband, Travis Barker.

On the Nov. 5 episode of "The Kardashians," the Poosh founder is seen driving around town with her close friend Simon Huck. She mentions that she received a phone call from her publicist, asking her to comment on previous speculation that Kardashian and the Blink-182 drummer were living separately and planning to split.

Kardashian called the claims "speculated rumors."

"I go, 'Tell them to print it. See what happens,'" she recalled.

"I was just like, 'They know it's not true. They know they saw a dumb TikTok of someone who makes up fabricated stories,'" Kardashian continued.

Huck agreed, noting he often sees "crazy, weird conspiracy theories" about Kardashian and her famous family.

"People believe it," Kardashian said of the gossip, which is sometimes fueled by AI-generated clips. It is "so bizarre."

Moments later, Kardashian, Huck, and her stepson, Landon Barker, pulled up to her longtime Calabasas home, which is undergoing a major renovation.

In the scene, Kardashian said she expected they were about six months away from being able to move back in. But she was beginning to second-guess that timeline after seeing how few changes had been made since her last visit.

"It's a really vulnerable feeling to have all of the walls of your home ripped apart. There's no electrical. There's nothing," she said in a confessional interview during the episode. "It's like a very vulnerable feeling."

While the renovation was in too early a stage to gather much about Kardashian's design plans, she did bring the cameras through a guest room that she said was going to be for Landon, before he moved into a place of his own.

The 23-year-old rapper was quick to pipe up and claim the space, however, saying that he'd love to have his own space because his current residence is a rental.

Barker and Kardashian have a large blended family of seven children. In November 2023, they welcomed a son, Rocky Thirteen Barker. Before their marriage in 2022, Kardashian had three children with her ex Scott Disick: Mason Disick, 16, Penelope Disick, 14, and Reign Disick, 11.

In addition to Landon, Barker and his ex Shanna Moakler are parents to a 20-year-old daughter, Alabama Barker. The musician also has a stepdaughter—Atiana De La Hoya, 27, whom Moakler had with boxer Oscar De La Hoya.

When asked if she was planning to house the couple's entire family under one roof, Kardashian appeared to have mixed feelings.

"I love it in theory, but I think it's really hard with everybody of all different ages," she said. "It's a much different balance."

Although all of the children may not end up living in the home full time, Kardashian confirmed there will be plenty of space for everyone to make memories in one way or another.

"We're working toward something amazing," she said. "I can't wait to cook and have movie nights, barbecues. I want to make it feel magical for everybody. It's so fun."

Kardashian and Barker have previously opened up about the arrangements they've made to suit their large family. In a 2024 episode of "The Kardashians," which was filmed when she was pregnant with Rocky in 2023, she discussed their plans to move into Barker's nearby Calabasas estate.

"We're going to move into his house, which is a block away, and then redo my house at the same time so that we can all be together, living under one roof with the baby," she said.

Barker opened the doors of that very house to Architectural Digest in May 2022, revealing a surprisingly laid-back aesthetic for a rocker whose vibe typically leans much edgier.

"I've had homes with lots of flashy cars, murals, and bikes hanging from the ceiling. But with three children of my own, plus Kourtney's kids, this place felt right for this moment in my life," he said. "I wanted a house where I can rest and enjoy my family, a place where we can create memories."

Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

The U.S. Treasury Department has moved to sever crypto exchange MEXC Global from the American financial system, accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in illicit transactions tied to Iran, North Korea and Russia.

In an order issued Friday through its Financial Crimes Enforcement Network, Treasury said MEXC, one of the world’s 20 largest crypto exchanges, served as a major conduit for sanctions evasion and cybercrime.

The action, part of a broader effort dubbed Operation Economic Outcast, bars U.S. financial institutions from processing any funds transfers to or from the exchange. It takes effect 30 days after publication in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in a statement, vowing to keep severing the regime’s “economic lifelines” and its attempts to use digital assets to dodge sanctions.

Founded in 2018, MEXC processes more than $1 billion in daily transactions, holds around $5 billion in reserves, and offers more than 3,000 tokens, according to FinCEN. Between May 2021 and May 2026, FinCEN said, the platform moved at least $16 billion connected to illicit activity, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion, with $232 million in direct transactions with sanctioned entities. The agency named MEXC a “primary money laundering concern” in connection with Russian illicit finance.

Despite publicly claiming anti-money-laundering controls and barring users in places like Iran and North Korea, MEXC’s compliance was riddled with “severe deficiencies,” FinCEN said.

The exchange was still transacting with sanctioned Russian exchange Garantex and its successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after Garantex was first designated. It also processed roughly $200 million linked to the A7A5 stablecoin and about $22 million benefiting Iran’s central bank and its Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

Those connections place MEXC squarely within a sprawling sanctions-evasion ecosystem that Treasury has been dismantling piece by piece.

The department last month designated Russia’s A7 Network a transnational criminal organization and proposed cutting its front companies off from U.S. finance, flagging more than $179 billion in transactions through its ruble-backed A7A5 token, much of it routed through Garantex and Grinex.

The same campaign has reached into Iran’s crypto sector, where Treasury gained sweeping authority to sanction anyone operating in it and blacklisted the Iranian exchange Nobitex over its role in funneling crypto to the IRGC.

The order uses a provision of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act rather than a standard sanctions designation, a tool FinCEN has increasingly reached for to cut off crypto infrastructure that operates outside the correspondent banking system.

With the ‘New’ Yeezy, Ye Says He Wants to ‘Let the Clothes Do the Talking.’ But Who’s Listening?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

“I don’t have a lot of words tonight,” Ye told me, not long after concluding his Yeezy Season 01 runway show in Los Angeles on Wednesday evening. “I’ll let the clothes do the talking.”

You might read that quote and be surprised to hear that Ye said it warmly, with an all-32-bared smile. But for those still paying attention, his relatively upbeat demeanor tracks with the tame-not-turnt persona that Ye has performed through most of 2026; the one that’s been slowly-but-surely trying to reamass the cultural capital he’s spent most of the last decade either annihilating or reclaiming. Legions of fans have proven they’re still all in; the culture at large remains justifiably dubious. The question this week is, how will Ye’s return to fashion affect the calculus?

Upon arrival, a typical Hollywood warehouse exterior turned into a Frank Herbert-esque interior as soon as you crossed the threshold. Inside, a hundred or so guests sat on two rows of unbroken benches that encircled a large white mound in the center, lit by a heavenly white glow from above. Inside was a mix of the usual cool kids: models (including the runway-favorite teen rapper Nettspend) and recurring characters in the overarching, enduring Kanye Universe. There was Fear of God founder Jerry Lorenzo, who made conversation with Don C, while 2 Chainz was patiently posted up in a hooded black leather coat like ’98 Wesley Snipes. Big Sean, rebuffing the sweltering indoor temperatures in a shearling jacket over a crewneck sweater, caught up with Hit-Boy.

Models on the Yeezy Season 01 runway, which featured a characteristically minimalist set piece.

The 19-year-old rapper Nettspend walked in the show.

Once everyone was seated, the lights dimmed as the unmistakable opening piano note of “Runaway” set the show into motion, with one lone model striding out in a killer black trench. From there, two more models, men this time, walked out in the opposite direction; what followed was a bit of organized chaos with the number of models and paths alternating at random intervals to the tune of a playlist that zipped between new Ye music (UK garage vibes, it would seem) and a gothic orchestra not unlike the scores Danny Elfman used to chef up for Tim Burton.

The fits were crisp, practical and tactical. Most of the menswear was ready-made for basic maneuvers, while the women’s looks were sleek and casual, with a few Milla Jovovich-in-Fifth-Element skimpy ensembles thrown in because Ye can only restrain himself but so much. The most notable thing, about the guys especially, were the relatively streamlined silhouettes: close-cropped coats, bombers and vests, fitted pants and sweats. Where past Ye lines championed and emblazoned the path for the baggy wave, with oversized boots and pants you could swim in, Ye seemed to be brazenly planting a flag back on the opposite end of the spectrum.

Nearly 12 years, and a dozen lifetimes, since his debut Yeezy Season 1 runway show in 2015, the notion of a “Season 01”—his first formal showing since his damning 2022 show in Paris, the one with the “White Lives Matter” shirts—is a conspicuous attempt to clear the slate. All of his previous shows had been produced in collaboration with Adidas, who severed ties with Ye four years ago, after he tweeted a tirade of antisemetic remarks. This time around, to let the clothes tell it, Ye is locked in. I don’t have an image of every single Yeezy Season line seared into my brain, but I feel comfortable saying Ye’s latest was the first time I looked at one of his collections and objectively thought, I could wear that. Most of the gear is quite solid—and familiar Ye's running the same playbook with Season 01 that he’s run with his music, and to some degree, his persona. It’s the Yeezy gear you know and love(d), refined to make it that much more palatable, similar to the way a song like “Whatever Works” puts him back over chipmunk’d soul samples. And true to the title, it does indeed; the fact that it does is maybe a testament to the brilliance that got lost in the insanity. But is playing the hits (quite literally, with “Runaway” opening the show) what we want from one of the most paradigm-shifting—and absolutely chaotic—creative minds of our time?

Historically, Ye’s designs are so focused on an idea of utilitarianism that they ultimately come off as a bit drab, if not downright dystopian. Essentials like hoodies and beefy leather jackets are still central to his vision, but as the models strode around in his usual earth-toned palette—save for a handful of black looks to start, and a bright pop of reds at the end—it became clear that Ye was putting on the same show in a different kind of stadium. The same sci-fi silhouettes, tweaked to their cleanest shape and most pleasing hue, sans screenprinted hate speech. Ideologically, the runway show wasn’t far off from the experience of hearing his 2007 hit “Good Life” all these years later, ringing off stadium speakers, carrying 80,000 people along on a wave of wistfulness— even if Ye himself only performed approximately 75% of the lyrics against a backing track.

In keeping with all of his other moves this year, Yeezy Season 01 is Ye presenting a safer, rule-abiding version of himself: Ye with just art, no antics. Much in the way that his latest album, Bully, eschewed any lyrics that say, shouted out R. Kelly or Bill Cosby (which is more than can be said about 2024’s Vultures 1), it felt like a deep sigh of relief to see him present clothes that don’t feature appalling slogans, at a fashion show with Candace Owens nowhere in sight. But the compromise for a sanitized Kanye is also one that can’t help but feel anodyne as well. The New Ye doesn’t needle, but he’s lost Old Ye’s knack for trying something new. Of course, we’re also well over a decade into the Yeezy experiment—at this point, the present has caught up to the futuristic vision Ye first dreamt up back in 2015. There’s a whole new generation of aspiring designers creating in his image, kids who fancy themselves trailblazers whose paths just loop back to Ye: streamer Kai Cenat just launched his own clothing brand, Vivet, while N3on attended Wednesday night’s show, skipping the Yeezy space gleefully documenting every interaction. It feels as though all of this has happened before, and all of it will happen again.

Mystery. Intrigue. Chaos. This is the typical life cycle for a Ye project, be it music, fashion, or otherwise. An announcement materializes, hype ensues. Then, inevitably, the seams start to show and it becomes apparent that he isn’t inviting you on a pre-planned journey so much as building the plane in real time, seconds before takeoff. And then, through sheer force of will, or maybe even genius, the result ends up so captivating that the speedbumps hardly matter. He’s still a master showman, and all it takes is a strong yearning for the Old Ye to find yourself surrendering to it. The efficacy of that last factor, though, has undeniably waned over the years. For much of the 2020s, in the midst of many a manic outburst or worse, Ye’s messes have hardly cohered into something objectively beautiful. The intrigue curdled into perverse bemusement; for every unwavering diehard fan, there was a rubbernecker tapped in to watch the circus.

The 2026 version of Ye has been an exercise in proving how many people will show up when he shuts up, and whether he can make it back from the brink if he delivers the right spectacle.

The result is the most well-oiled the Yeezy Machine has ever been. An album that more or less dropped when he said it would, for once. A smattering of live shows across the world, each in a sold-out stadium boasting record-breaking rap concert attendance where he leans on his most enduring strengths: visual spectacle and a bulletproof catalog. The only area left to renegotiate is fashion…which brings us back to Wednesday night in LA.

These are the scenes you’d expect at a Ye event, but the first sign that the overall vibe was different and more relaxed was that the man himself was at the door, tucked off into a hallway, immaculately dressed in a fitted all-black leather fit offset by a ridiculously large and ornate gold waist fixture—every bit the Dune-caliber fit as the space we were standing in required. Ahead of the show, he posed backstage flanked by his wife, Bianca Censori, and the four kids he shares with his ex, Kim Kardashian (including 13-year-old North, who was sporting her unmistakable blue-raspberry locks). At Yeezy events past, you’d expect to see harried staff scrambling, while Ye was somewhere out of sight but exuding stress and anxiety out into the atmosphere. But on Wednesday, the 49-year-old beamed as friends made their way past him to their seats. Even a three-piece-suited Kid Cudi came over to say hello.

Ye backstage with his children, from left: North, Psalm, Chicago, and Saint West.

Fifteen years ago, Yeezy Season 01 would’ve caused a massive sea change in the menswear world almost instantaneously, with the wave starting in SoHo then slowly emanating out into the beyond. In 2026, does Kanye still move that needle? The Year in Ye has been one big display of his ability to Don Draper the culture at large, and shock us all by “how much it never happened.” There’s no controversy big enough to keep him away for too long and very few fences he can’t mend.

For example, Sean and Cudi ceased their umpteenth falling out with their old label boss two months ago at a guest-packed, blockbuster homecoming show in Chicago on Ye’s tour stop; their show of support tonight only underlined how deeply the hatchet has been buried yet again. When I spoke to Big Sean after the show, he could only marvel at how much Ye has “given” to the culture across all different categories. One-time GOOD Music COO Steven Victor said the show made him feel like Ye “has his shit together,” noting that Ye admitted to him that he was still working out the model patterns hours before showtime. In past Yeezy Seasons, the cram-effect would’ve been apparent, but here, Victor said he wouldn’t have noticed because it all seemed to go off so effortlessly.

As for the man himself? I spoke to him, but again, he didn’t have much to say, at least on the record. He seemed excited that I picked up on the intentional disharmony of the model routes, and explained that the space we were standing in was a “language I’ve been working on for 10 years” called “DROAM,” which refers to the minimalist design he hopes to apply to stores, urban planning, and a home for himself.

“I got a quote for you,” Victor interjected. “The king returns!” Ye laughed. The question remains, though: Is he welcome?

US Treasury cuts off MEXC over alleged $16B in illicit transactions

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The US Treasury bureau FinCEN on Friday ordered US financial institutions to stop processing transfers involving MEXC Global.

The US Department of Treasury on Friday issued an order to cut cryptocurrency exchange MEXC Global off from the US’ financial system after accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in transactions linked to sanctions evasion, cybercrime and other illicit activity.

The Treasury’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN) asserted that MEXC handled transactions involving sanctioned groups in Iran, Russia and North Korea between May 2021 and May 2026. This included around $1.5 billion in transactions linked to Russian sanctions evasion, according to the official press release.

The order effectively bans US financial institutions from processing money transfers involving MEXC and will take effect 30 days after it is published in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” said Secretary of the Treasury Scott Bessent. “Treasury will continue to sever the Iranian regime’s economic lifelines and thwart its desperate attempts to circumvent U.S. sanctions using digital assets.”

The action is part of Operation Economic Outcast, a US Treasury campaign launched in August with aims to cut off financial entities that help Iran evade sanctions.

According to DefiLlama, the exchange held about $4.48 billion in total assets as of Friday. Cointelegraph contacted MEXC for comment but had not received a response by publication.

Will AI slop overwhelm creators? Mark Rober isn’t worried–but his team is using AI as a “thought partner” in production

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Mark Rober told Business Insider he thinks his content has a “moat” that will keep it safe from being affected by the AI slop deluge.

But, at the same time, he’s said his team is using generative AI as a “thought partner” for writing, and that it can make “the perfect meal” for students using his Class CrunchLabs modules.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

With BI, Rober addressed creators’ anxiety about AI-generated videos encroaching on the watch time that should go to their genuine content.

“Do you want to see a simulation of someone dunking a basketball with a sick trick, or do you want to see someone actually do it?” he told BI. “The fact that [viewers] know it’s real is what makes it crazy.”

He said creators should be reassured that “[o]ur brains developed for hundreds of thousands of years with human connection,” and viewers want “real and authentic” content filmed by people who interact with the world around them.

He also added that people need to retain their cognitive skills instead of just ChatGPTing everything. Both creators and viewers should investigate things themselves, using their own human brains.

“Curiosity is so important in this new age of AI,” he said. “It means you just have a lifetime of learning, versus like, ‘Oh, this thing knows the answer, so who cares?'”

But, again, Rober has implemented AI across his processes. This past June, he told The Information that AI “has the power to really adapt” educational settings.

“Basically what we’re doing with Class CrunchLabs is we’re making great ingredients, really fresh ingredients, and then AI can be like, ‘Hey, I have three kids in my class who are challenged this way, and they’re not great at reading, but these ones are. We’ve already covered this stuff.’ And it could take those ingredients and make the perfect meal for your class,” he explained. “I think it’s gonna be very good at that.”

He said that on the creator side, “I think it would allow me to scale myself in certain ways. We’re certainly using it as a thought partner on writing, on some thumbnail stuff.”

Like he did with BI, Rober said that in science content specifically, viewers probably want something real instead of something LLM’d. “I think it remains to be seen what the public’s appetite is for just pure AI-generated content,” he said. “I would think in science, you actually want to see the lava melt through the thing, versus it just being completely fake. But we’ll see.”

We’ve written before about how big tech platforms like YouTube have found themselves in a tricky catch-22: They want to make money off the AI bubble (uh oh), but as they roll out more generative tools and push creators to use them, they end up combating endless amounts of slop pumped out by those very same tools.

With Rober and other creators, we see something similar: They feel their content is safe from AI content made by other people, but also use AI in their processes, in the name of “scaling” or “ideating” or streamlining and cost-lessening production.

Rober’s team is using AI in its writing process, and a script is the foundation of long-form, educational YouTube content like his. Is the vibe just that viewers won’t care, so long as no fully AI-generated video makes it into an upload? Intriguing…

Aston Villa are reportedly preparing an offer to sign River Plate goalkeeper Santiago Beltran ahead of the January transfer window, as Unai Emery looks to strengthen his squad.

Such a development would significantly strengthen River Plate’s negotiating position and make a January transfer considerably more complicated.

It remains to be seen whether Aston Villa will follow through with their proposed offer and whether River Plate will entertain negotiations for their highly rated goalkeeper.

‘Zi’ Review: Kogonada Rediscovers His Voice with a Microbudget Hong Kong Experiment Starring Michelle Mao and Haley Lu Richardson

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Editor’s Note: This review was originally published during the 2026 Sundance Film Festival. Grasshopper Film releases “Zi” in select theaters starting Friday, November 6.

A Hong Kong violinist who’s losing her grip on reality, young Zi (Michelle Mao) is haunted by visions of her future self. The doctors at the local neurology center suspect that she might be suffering from the early symptoms of a brain tumor, but Zi — a being of pure cinema, cobbled together from the half-formed memories of films like “La Jetée” and “Cléo from 5 to 7” — will have to wait a long and lonely 24 hours for the test results to come back.  Related Stories ‘To Hold a Mountain’ Took on the Government — Now It’s Montenegro’s Oscar Submission ‘Soul Patrol’ Review: The Vietnam War’s First Black Special Ops Team Shares Their Stories in An Enlightening Documentary

Well, maybe not so lonely. Crying on one of the innumerable concrete steps that climb Lantau Island, Zi is consoled by a sweet American expat with a strangely familiar face (and an aggressively unnatural blonde wig). Her name is L, she’s a disillusioned former dancer played by the luminous Haley Lu Richardson, and she can’t help but reciprocate the inexplicable closeness that Zi feels towards her. Over the course of a single night together, these two lost souls will help to relocate each other in time — to resynchronize with the world around them, reconnect with the infinite potential of the present, and return themselves to the creative purpose that once anchored them to it.

Even if seen without a shred of context, it would be obvious to anyone that “Zi” was made by someone who is — or hopefully was — desperate to spark a realignment of his own. To steal himself away from regret and anxiety and tether his perspective back to the now. Shot without a script or a budget in the immediate aftermath of Kogonada’s ill-received “A Big Bold Beautiful Journey” (the “After Yang” auteur’s studio debut, by all indications a soul-crushing experience for an artist whose two previous features retained the delicate grace of the video essays that he used to make), this searching and unformed wisp of an experiment hardly represents the first time that an ascendent director has scaled down after a misstep, but “Zi” takes the notion of “one for me” to much greater and more galvanizing extremes than usual.

This isn’t just a filmmaker trying to get back on track, it’s a filmmaker desperately, nakedly searching for a way to fall back in love with filmmaking itself. A way to prove that it might still love him back. (Kogonada described the project to IndieWire as “A retreat, as well as a pursuit.”)

While the prospect of watching Kogonada do that will naturally be of greater interest to those who have watched him get to this point, this movie’s palpably improvised nature — its stolen shots and recursive shape lend the impression of someone trying to cut their ultra-evocative vacation footage into a coherent narrative — would force anyone to wonder about the meta-textual aspects of its creation. “You feel like your sole purpose in life is something, and it doesn’t choose you back,” L sputters out at one point. “What do you do with that? I had to get away from it all. That’s why I came here. Wandering.”

Since arriving in Hong Kong, she’s made a habit of collecting sounds in much the same way as Kogonada has come there on the hunt for images. They both find a dissonance around Victoria Harbor that reflects their internal feelings of otherness and self-alienation. It’s China, but not. It’s a single city, but one split across two main areas by a stretch of the sea that some people never cross. It’s among the world’s most densely populated metropolises, but the vertical terrain seems fundamentally inhospitable to human civilization, and nature continues to assert itself between every skyscraper, shopping mansion, and crack in the sidewalk. Zi is the only native character in the film, but the history and design of Hong Kong reflects her disassociation as well — like her, the former British colony’s experience of the present is delayed.

That’s the closest thing to a diagnosis we get for what ails her, though it’s clear that Kogonada is leaning more towards soft science-fiction than the stuff of hard medicine. “Temoral relativism” is how L’s ex-fiancé Min (Jin Ha) describes it, though he’s slow to reveal how much he knows about Zi’s condition. Everything she perceives is already in the past. I suppose that’s true for all of us to some degree, if you factor in the nanoseconds that elapse between stimuli and our ability to perceive it, but the situation is more pronounced for Zi (who Mao plays with the effervescent helplessness of a paper airplane). Of course, that doesn’t explain why she believes she’s bearing witness to visions of her future self, but that confusion only exacerbates her feelings of distance from the present.

Even in its more platonic dimensions, the threadbare story that gradually allows Zi to close that gap is awash with the romance of loss, reconciliation, and belonging. Kogonada is falling back in love with the movies right before our eyes, and yet,  in characteristic fashion, that process isn’t garish or forced. His is a vision that is revealed through careful subtraction — by clearing away all of the dust and smudges that came to obscure it.

While Kogonada indulges in a few legible winks to his cinematic forebears (the most obvious of those being a step-printed homage to Wong Kar Wai, and my most far-fetched being a disoriented nod to José Luis Guerín’s “In the City of Sylvia”), he and cinematographer Benjamin Loeb almost completely eschew the neon exoticism that foreigners tend to look for in Hong Kong, with the vast majority of the film being captured in unfussy handheld shots that accentuate its here-and-now anxiety and delegate the emotional heavy lifting to an impeccably curated soundtrack of Ryuichi Sakamoto remixes and originals.

By the end, “Zi” engenders a certain emotional attachment toward and between its characters, all of it building to a gently unexpected gutpunch of a final minute. But even that last-minute reveal — not a plot twist so much as a sudden change in perspective — is rooted more in the movie’s form than its plot, as Kogonada’s characters remain largely symbolic of their function in the writer-director’s journey back to himself.

It’s a testament to Mao, Ha, and especially Richardson — who continues to be so good at grounding Kogonada’s most abstract ideas in a tender human place — that “Zi” retains even a soft dramatic pull as it searches for the present, though its recursive nature certainly wasn’t conceived with a casual audience in mind. If you’re hooked, which I wasn’t, or haunted by it, which I was, that will likely have less to do with an acute emotional connection to these characters than with the overflowing rewards of watching someone rediscover the sound of their own voice, and hear a way forward into the future in its echoes.

“Zi” premiered at the 2026 Sundance Film Festival. Grasshopper Film releases it in select theaters starting Friday, November 6.

The Yangwang U9 Xtreme Just Set Two Guinness World Speed Records

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The electric Chinese supercar went more than 312 mph on a public road in the Abu Dhabi desert.

The fastest production car in the world. No, not necessarily an attribute that would be useful in your real life, unless your driveway is a section of unrestricted autobahn, but as an achievement, it's something automakers love to boast about. Porsche. Ferrari. Bugatti. And now Yangwang.

The luxury division of Chinese giant BYD (as Lexus is to Toyota), Yangwang's appropriately named U9 Xtreme hypercar just hit 312 mph on a public road in Abu Dhabi. This feat nets it two Guinness World Records at once: the single-direction highest speed for both a production car and for a production electric car.

At this point, perhaps the electric production speed record doesn't need its own subdivision, especially since the U9 was chiefly breaking its own previous record of 308 mph, set last year. The U9 Xtreme, or U9X, has four electric motors, one at each wheel, for a grand total of over 3000 hp. That's three Veyrons concentrated into a single car.

See all results for used cars for sale near 99504

The battery architecture is also distinct from the standard U9, with a 1200-volt architecture and the ability to both send and receive huge amounts of power with greatly improved thermal efficiency. Even when nearly depleted, at 20 percent charge, it can still pump out 1800 kilowatts of power (2414 hp), or soak up 700 kilowatts through regenerative braking.

A motor at each corner means almost limitless fine-tuning for torque-vectoring control, meting out all that power with minimal wheelslip. The U9X also features electric dampers that clamp down on squat and dive under hard acceleration and braking, and active aerodynamic elements to maintain stability at speed. A tip of the hat also goes to Indonesia's Giti Tires, who developed the rubber capable of putting up with these tremendous speeds and forces.

Yangwang only plans to build thirty of these U9X models, and it's not like you can just slap on a license plate and bully 911 Turbos on the Autobahn. The 4.35-mile stretch of public road used for the record attempt was closed at the time, and also fastidiously cleaned of debris, including going back over the surface looking for any wires left by the street sweepers.

However, as a demonstration of just how advanced BYD's battery chemistry can be, this new speed record represents all kinds of bragging rights. Speed records come and go. For now, nothing can touch the Yangwang U9 Xtreme.

Treasury cuts off MEXC Global over alleged $16 billion in transactions linked to Iran, North Korea and Russia

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued an order against MEXC Global and said it was a money laundering concern.

The U.S. Treasury Department says it has severed crypto exchange MEXC Global from the United States after it allegedly supported $16 billion in transactions connected to Iran, North Korea, and Russia.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued an order against MEXC Global and said it was a money laundering concern.

"Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system," said Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent in a statement. "Treasury will continue to sever the Iranian regime’s economic lifelines and thwart its desperate attempts to circumvent U.S. sanctions using digital assets."

Over the past year, Treasury has implemented a crackdown of sorts on crypto platforms allegedly helping Iran evade sanctions amid its ongoing war against the country. In June, Iran's largest crypto exchange, Nobitex, was sanctioned after Treasury said it processed more than half of Iran's crypto inflows in 2025. In September, the U.S. also sanctioned Iranian crypto exchange BitBank over alleged bitcoin transfers to the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

On Friday, Treasury said that though MEXC Global said it maintained anti-money laundering and says it will block the use of people using it in countries including Iran and North Korea, that it had "severe deficiencies" in those AML controls. The department said Friday's sanction will protect the U.S. financial system.

"Today's action will protect the U.S. financial system from the significant illicit finance threats posed by MEXC Global," the department said.
